# NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament

"NCAA men's basketball tournament" redirects here. For other division tournaments, see [NCAA basketball tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_basketball_tournament_\(disambiguation\)).

"March Madness" redirects here. For other uses, see [March Madness (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_Madness_\(disambiguation\)).

"NCAA March Madness" redirects here. For its televised coverage, see [NCAA March Madness (TV program)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_March_Madness_\(TV_program\)). For the video game series formerly known as NCAA March Madness, see [NCAA Basketball (series)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Basketball_\(series\)).

"The Big Dance" redirects here. For other uses, see [Big Dance (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Dance_\(disambiguation\)).

The **NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament**, branded as **March Madness**, or **The Big Dance**, is a [single-elimination tournament](939680.md) played in the United States to determine the men's [college basketball](768856.md) national champion of the [Division I](669931.md) level in the [National Collegiate Athletic Association](60706.md) (NCAA). Played mostly during March, the tournament was first conducted in [1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1939_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and currently consists of 76 teams. Known for its [upsets](1169845.md) of favored teams with its single game [single-elimination](939680.md) format, it has become one of the most popular annual sporting events in the US.

The current 76-team format will begin with the next edition of the tournament in [2027](81569697.md); the tournament format had remained largely unchanged since [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), when it expanded to 64 teams. Before then, the size of the field varied from as little as 8 teams to as many as 53. The field was restricted to conference champions until at-large bids were extended in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) and teams were not fully seeded until [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament). In [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the tournament was cancelled for the first time due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) and in the next season, the [tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) was contested completely in the state of [Indiana](21883857.md) as a precaution.

37 schools have won the tournament. [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball) has the most with 11 championships and their coach [John Wooden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Wooden) has the most titles of any coach with 10. The [University of Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball) (UK) has eight championships, the [University of Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UConn_Huskies_men's_basketball) (UConn) and the [University of North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_men's_basketball) have six championships, [Duke University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_men's_basketball) and [Indiana University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_Hoosiers_men's_basketball) have five championships, the [University of Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_Jayhawks_men's_basketball) (KU) has four championships and the [University of Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Gators_men's_basketball) and [Villanova University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villanova_Wildcats_men's_basketball) have three titles. The [University of Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball) has won three national championships but one has been vacated. Six other programs are tied with two national championships and 21 teams have won it once.

All tournament games are broadcast by [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS), [TBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TBS_\(American_TV_channel\)), [TNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TNT_\(American_TV_network\)) and [truTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TruTV) under the program name *[NCAA March Madness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_March_Madness_\(TV_program\))*. With a contract through 2032, [Paramount Skydance](79917576.md) and [Warner Bros. Discovery](67824355.md) pay \$891 million annually for the broadcast rights. The NCAA distributes revenue to participating teams based on how far they advance, which provides significant funding for college athletics. The tournament has become part of American popular culture through [bracket contests that award money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_Madness_pools) and other prizes for correctly predicting the outcomes of the most games. In 2023, *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)* reported that an estimated 60 to 100 million brackets are filled out each year.

## History

### Early era (1939–1970)

The first tournament was held in [1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1939_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and was won by [Oregon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938%E2%80%9339_Oregon_Webfoots_men's_basketball_team). It was the idea of [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_State_Buckeyes_men's_basketball) coach [Harold Olsen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harold_Olsen). The [National Association of Basketball Coaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Basketball_Coaches) operated the first tournament for the NCAA.&#x20;

From 1939 to 1950, the NCAA tournament consisted of eight teams, with each selected from a geographical district. Multiple conferences were considered part of each district, such as the [Missouri Valley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Valley_Conference) and the [Big Seven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Eight_Conference) conferences in one district and the [Southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Conference) and [Southeastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference) conferences in another, which often led to top-ranked teams being left out of the tournament. The issue came to a head in [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_NCAA_basketball_tournament), when the NCAA suggested that third-ranked [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949%E2%80%9350_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team) and fifth-ranked [North Carolina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949%E2%80%9350_NC_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball_team) compete in a playoff game for a bid, but Kentucky refused, believing they should be given the bid as the higher-ranked team. In response, the NCAA doubled the field to 16 in [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_NCAA_basketball_tournament), adding two additional districts and six spots for at-large teams. Conferences could still only have one team in the tournament, but multiple conferences from the same geographic district could now be included through at-large bids. This development helped the NCAA compete with the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Invitation_Tournament) for prestige.[^cite_note-UP1951ScandalElevates-2]

In the eight team format, the tournament was split into the East and West Regions, with champions meeting in the national championship game. The first two rounds for each region were conducted at the same site and the national championship and, from [1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_NCAA_basketball_tournament), consolation game occurred a week later. Some years, the site of the national championship was the same site as a regional championship and in other years a new site. With the expansion to 16 teams, the tournament retained the original format of the national semifinals being the regional finals in [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_NCAA_basketball_tournament). For the [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_NCAA_basketball_tournament) tournament, there were four regions named East-1, East-2, West-1, West-2, all played at separate sites. The regional champions met for the national semifinals and championship at a separate location a week later, establishing the format with two final rounds of the tournament (although the name "Final Four" would not be used in branding until the 1980s).

The [1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_NCAA_basketball_tournament) tournament expanded to include 22 teams and added a fifth round, with ten teams receiving a bye to the regional semifinals. The number of teams would fluctuate from 22 to 25 teams over the next two decades, but the number of rounds remained the same. The double region naming was kept until [1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_NCAA_basketball_tournament), when the regions were named the East, Midwest, West, and Far West. In [1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game), the regions were named East, Mideast, Midwest, and West, which remained until 1985. Regions were paired in the national semifinals based on their geographic locations, with the two eastern regions meeting in one semifinal and two western regions meeting in the other semifinal.

Beginning in [1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_NCAA_basketball_tournament), a national third-place game was held before the championship game. Regional third-place games were played in the West from [1939](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1939_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and the East from [1941](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1941_NCAA_basketball_tournament). Despite expansion in [1951](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1951_NCAA_basketball_tournament), there were still only two regions, each with a third-place game. The [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_NCAA_basketball_tournament) tournament had four regions each with a third-place game.

This era of the tournament was characterized by competition with the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Invitation_Tournament). Founded by the Metropolitan Basketball Writers Association one year before the NCAA tournament, the NIT was held entirely in New York City at Madison Square Garden. Because New York was the center of the press in the United States, the NIT often received more coverage than the NCAA tournament in early years. Additionally, good teams were often excluded from the NCAA tournament because each conference could only have one bid and conference champions were even excluded because of the 8-district system before 1950. Teams often competed in both tournaments during the first decade, with [City College of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949%E2%80%9350_CCNY_Beavers_men's_basketball_team) winning both the [NIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_National_Invitation_Tournament) and [NCAA tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_NCAA_basketball_tournament) in 1950. Soon after, the NCAA banned teams from participating in both tournaments.

### Pre-modern era (1971–1984)

Two major changes over the course of the early 1970s led to the NCAA becoming the preeminent post-season tournament for college basketball. First, the NCAA added a rule in [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) that banned teams who declined an invitation to the NCAA tournament from participating in other post-season tournaments. This was in response to eighth-ranked [Marquette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969%E2%80%9370_Marquette_Warriors_men's_basketball_team) declining its invitation in [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) and instead participating in and winning the [NIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_National_Invitation_Tournament) after coach [Al McGuire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_McGuire) complained about their regional placement. Since then, the NCAA tournament has clearly been the major one, with conference champions and the majority of the top-ranked teams participating.[^cite_note-3] Second, the NCAA allowed multiple teams per conference starting in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament). This was in response to several highly ranked teams being denied bids during the early 1970s. These included [South Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969%E2%80%9370_South_Carolina_Gamecocks_men's_basketball_team) in 1970, which was undefeated in conference play but lost in the ACC tournament; second-ranked [USC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970%E2%80%9371_USC_Trojans_men's_basketball_team) in [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), which was left out because their conference was represented by top-ranked [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969%E2%80%9370_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team); and [Maryland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973-74_Maryland_Terrapins_men's_basketball_team) in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament), which was ranked #3 but lost the [ACC tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_ACC_men's_basketball_tournament) championship game to eventual national champion [North Carolina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973%E2%80%9374_NC_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball_team).[^cite_note-4]

To accommodate at-large bids, the tournament expanded in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) to include 32 teams, allowing a second team to represent a conference in addition to the conference champion,[^cite_note-2024MFFBook-5] and eliminated byes. In [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_championship_game), the tournament expanded to 40 teams and added a sixth round; 24 teams received byes to the second round. Eight more teams were added in [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) with only 16 teams receiving byes, and the restriction on the number of at-large bids from a conference was removed.[^cite_note-2024MFFBook-5] In [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), a seventh round with four play-in games was added; an additional play-in game was added in [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). Beginning in [1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), the regional pairings for the national semifinals were rotated on a yearly basis instead of the two eastern and two western regions always playing.

Seeding also began during this era, adding drama and ensuring better teams had better paths to the Final Four. In [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament), teams were seeded in two separate pools based on their qualification method. Each region had four teams which automatically qualified ranked Q1–Q4 and four teams which received an at-large bid ranked L1–L4. In [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament), all teams in each region were seeded 1 through 10, without regards for their qualification method.

The national semifinals were moved to Saturday and the championship was moved to Monday evening in [1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), where they have remained since. Until then, the championship had been played on Saturday and the semifinals two days before.

The third-place games were eliminated during this era, with the last regional third-place games played in [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) and the last national third-place game played in [1981](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament).

### Modern era (1985–present)

In [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the tournament expanded to 64 teams, eliminating all byes and play-ins. For the first time, all teams had to win six games to win the tournament. This expansion led to increased media coverage and popularity in American culture. Until [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the First and Second Rounds occurred at two sites in each region.

In 1985, the Mideast Region was renamed the Southeast Region. In [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the Southeast Region became the South Region. From [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) to [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the regions were named after their host cities, e.g. the Phoenix regional in 2004, the Chicago regional in 2005, and the Minneapolis regional in 2006, but reverted to the traditional geographic designations beginning in [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). For the 2011 tournament, the South Region was the Southeast Region and the Midwest Region the Southwest Region; both returned to their previous names in 2012.

The [1996 Final Four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_Final_Four) was the last to take place in a venue built specifically for basketball. Since then, the Final Four has exclusively been played in large indoor football stadiums.

Beginning in [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the field was expanded from 64 to 65 teams, adding to the tournament what was informally known as the "play-in game". This was in response to the creation of the [Mountain West Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_West_Conference) during 1999. Originally, the winner of the [Mountain West's tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_West_Conference_men's_basketball_tournament) did not receive an automatic bid, due to standard NCAA rules regarding new conferences and automatic bids. As an alternative to eliminating an at-large bid, the NCAA expanded the tournament to [65 teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). The #64 and #65 seeds were seeded in a regional bracket as 16 seeds, and then played the opening round game on the Tuesday preceding the first weekend of the tournament. This game was always played at the [University of Dayton Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UD_Arena) in Dayton, Ohio.

Starting in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the selection committee revealed the overall rankings among the #1 seeds. Based on these rankings, the regions were paired so that the #1 overall seed would play the #4 overall seed in a national semifinal if both teams made the Final Four. This was to prevent the top two teams from meeting before the finals, as was largely considered the case in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) when Kentucky played Massachusetts in the Final Four. Previously, regional pairings rotated yearly.

In [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), there was speculation about increasing the tournament size to as many as 128 teams. On April 1, 2010, the NCAA announced that it was looking at expanding to 96 teams for [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). However, three weeks later the NCAA announced a new television contract with CBS/Turner that expanded the field to 68 teams, instead of 96, starting in 2011. The First Four was created by the addition of three play-in games.[^cite_note-sports.espn.go.com-6] Two of the First Four games pit 16 seeds against each other. The two other games, however, pit the last at-large bids against each other. The seeding for the at-large teams will be determined by the selection committee and fluctuates based on the true seed ranking of the teams. Explaining the reasoning for this format, selection committee chairman [Dan Guerrero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Guerrero) said, "We felt if we were going to expand the field it would create better drama for the tournament if the First Four was much more exciting. They could all be on the 10 line or the 12 line or the 11 line."[^cite_note-sports.espn.go.com-6] As part of this expansion, the round of 64 was renamed the second round and the round of 32 was renamed the Third Round, with the First Four being officially the First Round.[^cite_note-sports.espn.go.com-6] In [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the rounds of 64 and 32 returned to their previous names of the first round and the second round and the First Four became the official name of the opening round.[^cite_note-7]

In [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the NCAA introduced a new "NCAA March Madness" logo for tournament-wide branding, which included bespoke tourney-exclusive courts at each of the tournament venues from the same manufacturer with the same floor composition across each round. Previously, the NCAA would use the venue's existing default floor (be it solely for college basketball or a dual-use [NBA](22093.md)/college floor) with tournament and NCAA decals applied.

Beginning in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the #1 overall seed picks the sites for their first- and second-round games and their potential regional games. Additionally, the selection committee began releasing the top 16 seeds three weeks before Selection Sunday as a bracket preview.

Due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](63136490.md), the NCAA cancelled the [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) tournament. Initially, the NCAA discussed holding a shortened version with only 16 teams in the Final Four host city of Atlanta. Once the vast scale of the pandemic was understood, the NCAA cancelled the tournament, making it the first edition not to be held, and decided against releasing the brackets that the Selection Committee had been working on.

In [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), the tournament was held entirely in the state of Indiana to reduce travel. This was to date the only time the tournament was conducted in one state. As a COVID-19 precaution, all participating teams were required to stay in NCAA-provided accommodations until they lost. The schedule was adjusted to provided extended time for COVID-19 evaluation before the tournament began, with the First Four occurring entirely on Thursday, the First and Second Rounds pushed one day back to a Friday-Monday window, and the Sweet Sixteen and Elite Eight pushed to a Friday-Monday window as well. Teams ranked 69–72 by the Selection Committee were put on "standby" to replace any team that withdrew from the tournament due to COVID-19 protocols during the 48 hours after the brackets were announced. Only one game was declared a no contest due to COVID-19, with Oregon advancing to the second round because VCU could not participate due to COVID-19 protocols. VCU was not replaced by one of the first four teams out because the COVID-19 infections started more than two days after the brackets were announced. The tournament returned to its regular format in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament).

In response to [protests from players in the 2021 women's tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_tournament#Facilities_inequality) about the differing facility quality and branding, both the [men's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) and [women's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_tournament) tournaments were branded as "NCAA March Madness" starting in 2022 with variations of the same tournament-wide logo used by the men's tournament. Additionally, the Final Four for the men's tournament was branded as the "Men's Final Four" beginning in 2022, reflecting the "Women's Final Four" branding in use for that tournament since [1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NCAA_Division_I_women's_basketball_tournament).

In 2023, the NCAA Division I Transformation Committee published a report that recommended expanding the tournament to include 25% of all competing teams in Division I, which would amount to roughly 90 teams.[^cite_note-8] Discussions within the NCAA took place over the next two years to potentially expend the tournament to 72 or 76 teams.[^cite_note-9] On May 7, 2026, the NCAA officially voted to expand the men's and women's tournaments to 76 teams beginning with the [2027](81569697.md) edition. Under the new format, the First Four will expand from featuring eight teams in four games to 24 teams in 12 games. Of the 24 First Four teams, 12 will be the lowest-seeded at-large teams, and the other 12 will be the lowest-seeded automatic qualifying teams. On July 10, 2026, it was announced that [Wichita, Kansas](114932.md), alongside Dayton, Ohio (which has hosted the First Four games since 2001), will host these Opening Round games in 2027 and 2028.[^cite_note-10][^cite_note-11][^cite_note-12][^cite_note-13]

**Notes**

## Format



A ticket from the 1988 tournament held in Kansas City, Missouri

The tournament consists of 76 teams competing in seven rounds of a [single-elimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-elimination) bracket. Thirty-two teams automatically qualify for the tournament by winning their conference tournament, played during the two weeks before the tournament, and forty-four teams qualify by receiving an [at-large bid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/At-large_bid) based on their performance during the season.[^cite_note-17] The selection committee determines the at-large bids, ranks all the teams 1 to 76, and places the teams in the bracket, all of which is revealed publicly on the Sunday before the tournament, dubbed "[Selection Sunday](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_basketball_tournament_selection_process#Selection_Sunday)" by the media and fans. There is no reseeding during the tournament and matchups in each subsequent round are predetermined by the bracket.

The tournament is divided into four regions, with each region having sixteen to eighteen teams. Regions are named after the U.S. geographic area of the city hosting each regional semifinal and regional final (the tournament's third and 4th round overall). Host cities for all regions vary from year to year.

The tournament is played over three weekends, with two rounds occurring each weekend. Before the first weekend, 24 teams compete in the Opening Round to advance to the first round. Six games pair the lowest-ranked conference champions and Six games pair the lowest-ranked at-large qualifiers. The first and second rounds are played during the first weekend, the regional semifinals and regional finals during the second weekend, and the national semifinals and national championship game during the third weekend. Regional rounds are branded as the Sweet Sixteen and Elite Eight and the third weekend is branded as the Final Four, all named after the number of teams remaining at the beginning of the round. All games, including the Opening Round, are scheduled so that teams will have one rest day between each game. This format has been in use since 2011, with minor changes to the schedule in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md).

| Round | Teams  Remaining | Teams  Competing | Games | Week | Dates |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Opening round (play-in round) | 76 | 24 | 12 | 1st | Tuesday & Wednesday |
| First round | 64 | 64 | 32 | Thursday & Friday |  |
| Second round | 32 | 32 | 16 | Saturday & Sunday |  |
| Sweet Sixteen (regional semifinals) | 16 | 16 | 8 | 2nd | Thursday & Friday |
| Elite Eight (regional finals) | 8 | 8 | 4 | Saturday & Sunday |  |
| Final Four (national semifinals) | 4 | 4 | 2 | 3rd | Saturday |
| National championship game | 2 | 2 | 1 | Monday |  |

### Seeding and bracket

Further information: [NCAA basketball tournament selection process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_basketball_tournament_selection_process)

The Selection Committee, which includes conference commissioners and university [athletic directors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athletic_director) appointed by the NCAA, determines the bracket during the week before the tournament. Since the results of several conference tournaments occurring during the same week can significantly impact the bracket, the Committee often makes several brackets for different results.

To make the bracket, the Committee ranks the whole field from 1 to 76; these are referred to as the *true seed*. The committee then divides the teams amongst the four regions, giving each a seed between No. 1 and No. 16. The same four seeds in all the regions are referred to as the *seed line* (i.e. the No. 6 seed line). 24 teams are doubled up and compete in the First Four. Eight of the paired teams compete for the No. 16 seeds, four of the paired teams compete for the No. 15 teams, and the other 12 paired teams are the last at-large teams awarded bids to the tournament and compete for a seed line in the No. 10 to No. 14 range, which varies year to year based on the true seeds of the teams overall.[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18]

The top four overall seeds are placed as No. 1 seeds in each region. The regions are paired so that if all the No. 1 seeds reached the Final Four true seed No. 1 would play No. 4 and No. 2 would play No. 3. The No. 2 teams are preferably placed so that the No. 5 true seed will not be paired with the No. 1 true seed. The committee ensures competitive balance among the top four seeds in each region by adding the true seed values up and comparing the values among the regions. If there is significant deviation, some teams will be moved among the regions to balance the true seed distribution.[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18]

If a conference has two to four teams in the top four seeds, they will be placed in different regions. Otherwise, teams from the same conference are placed to avoid a rematch before the regional finals if they have played three or more times in the season, the regional semifinals if they have played twice, or the second round if they have played once. Additionally, the committee is advised to avoid rematches from the regular season and the previous years' tournament in the First Four. Finally, the committee will attempt to ensure that a team is not moved out of their preferred geographical region an inordinate number of times based on their placement in the previous two tournaments. To follow these rules and preferences, the committee may move a team off of their expected seed line. Thus, for example, the 40th overall ranked team, originally slated to be a No. 10 seed within a particular region, may instead be moved up to a No. 9 seed or moved down to a No. 11 seed.[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18]

Since 2012, the committee has released the No. 1 to 68 true seed list after announcing the bracket.[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18] Since 2017, the Selection Committee has released a list of the top 16 teams three weeks before Selection Sunday. This list does not guarantee any team a bid, as the Committee re-ranks all teams when starting the final selection process.[^cite_note-19]



The [University of Dayton Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UD_Arena), which has hosted all First Four games since the round's inception in 2011 (except 2021), as well as its precursor, the single "play-in" game held from 2001 to 2010. As of 2026, the arena has hosted 143 tournament games, the most of any venue.

The seed line of the four at-large teams competing in the First Four has varied each year, depending on the overall ranking of the at-large teams in the field.[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18]

| Seed | Count | Years |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 10 | 2 | 2024 (×2) |
| 11 | 18 | 2011, 2013, 2014, 2015–2019 (×2), 2021 (×2), 2022, 2023 (×2), 2025 (x2), 2026 (x2) |
| 12 | 4 | 2011, 2012, 2014, 2022 |
| 13 | 1 | 2013 |
| 14 | 1 | 2012 |

### Venues

In the men's tournament, all sites are nominally neutral; teams are prohibited from playing tournament games on their home courts during the first, second, and regional rounds. Under NCAA rules, any court on which a team hosts more than three regular-season games (not including preseason or conference tournament games) is considered a "home court".[^cite_note-NCAABracketPrinciples-18] For the First Four and the Final Four, the home court prohibition does not apply because only one venue hosts these rounds. The First Four is regularly hosted by the [Dayton Flyers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dayton_Flyers_men's_basketball); as such, the team competed on their home court in [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament).[^cite_note-20] Because the Final Four is hosted at indoor football stadiums, it is unlikely that a team will play on their home court in the future. The last time this was possible was in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) when the [Continental Airlines Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Airlines_Arena), home court of [Seton Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seton_Hall_Pirates_men's_basketball), hosted.

For the first and second rounds, eight venues host games, four on each day of the round. Each venue hosts two sets of four teams, referred to as "pods." To limit travel, teams are placed in pods closer to their home unless seeding rules would prevent it. Because each pod includes a top 4 seed, the highest ranked teams normally get the closest sites.

The possible pods by seeding are:

- Pod A: 1 v. 16 and 8 v. 9

- Pod B: 2 v. 15 and 7 v. 10

- Pod C: 3 v. 14 and 6 v. 11

- Pod D: 4 v. 13 and 5 v. 12

*Schools that have won the NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament*\

## Appearances

Main article: [Bids by school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament_bids_by_school)

A total of 333 teams have appeared in the NCAA tournament since 1939. Because the NCAA did not split into divisions until [1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game), some schools that have appeared in the tournament are no longer in [Division I](669931.md). Among Division I schools, 46 have never made the tournament, including 11 that are ineligible because they are transitioning to Division I.

### Results by year

**Key**

- &#x20;**CH**  National Champion

- &#x20;RU  National Runner-up

- &#x20;F4  Final Four

- &#x20;E8  Elite Eight

- &#x20;16  Sweet Sixteen (*Began in 1951.*)

- &#x20;32  Round of 32 (*Began in 1975.*)&#x20;

  - &#x20;22   23   24   25  Round of 22–25 (*Between 1953 and 1974, between 22 and 25 teams were invited to the tournament.*)

- &#x20;✖  Round of 64 (*Began in 1979. Fewer than 64 teams invited before 1985.*)

- &#x20;ƒ  Play-In Round (*1983–1984 and 2001–2010*), First Four (*2011–2026*), Opening Round (*2027–present*)

- &#x20;~~**CH**~~   ~~RU~~   ~~F4~~   ~~E8~~   ~~16~~   ~~32~~   ~~✖~~   ~~ƒ~~  The team achieved the placement shown, but the participation was later vacated. These vacated appearances are not included in the totals columns, with two exceptions.

  [^cite_note-21]

  - After the [University of Michigan basketball scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Michigan_basketball_scandal), [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michigan_Wolverines_men's_basketball)'s appearance in the [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) Final Four and Championship Game were vacated, but not their entire participation in the tournament. Therefore, Michigan is listed as  ~~RU~~  in 1992, but the Wolverines credited with an appearance, a Sweet Sixteen, and an Elite Eight in the totals columns.

  - After the [Syracuse University athletics scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_University_athletics_scandal), [Syracuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syracuse_Orange_men's_basketball) forfeited games across multiple seasons, including 2004–05, 2006–07, 2010–11, and 2011–12, three of which included an NCAA Tournament appearance. However, the NCAA allowed Syracuse to keep their NCAA appearances in the record book, so those seasons are not marked with strikethrough, and are fully included in the totals columns.

Programs are listed below under their current athletic branding, which is not necessarily what was used at the time. For example, [Oklahoma State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_State_Cowboys_men's_basketball) was known as "Oklahoma A\&M" when it won back-to-back championships in 1945 and 1946, and [UTEP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UTEP_Miners_men's_basketball) was known as "Texas Western" when it won the national championship in 1966.

For each season starting in 1978, the team's seed is shown in superscript to the left of the result.

## Rituals and influence

### National anthem

During the Final Four game, the national anthem was sung by four student-athletes from the four semi-finalist schools with one student-athlete representing each school.&#x20;

### Cutting down the nets

Main article: [Cutting down the nets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cutting_down_the_nets)



The NABC championship trophy



NCAA-style trophies for various sports, as seen at [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLA_Bruins)

As a tournament ritual, the winning team [cuts down the nets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cutting_down_the_nets) at the end of regional championship games as well as the national championship game. Starting with the seniors, and moving down by classes, players each cut a single strand off each net; the head coach cuts the last strand connecting the net to the hoop, claiming the net itself.[^cite_note-23] An exception to the head coach cutting the last strand came in [2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), when [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%9313_Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball_team) head coach [Rick Pitino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Pitino) gave that honor to [Kevin Ware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Ware), who had suffered a catastrophic leg injury during the tournament.[^cite_note-24] This tradition is credited to [Everett Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everett_Case), the coach of [North Carolina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Carolina_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball), who stood on his players' shoulders to accomplish the feat after the Wolfpack won the Southern Conference tournament in 1947.[^cite_note-25] CBS, since 1987 and yearly to 2015, in the odd-numbered years since 2017, and TBS, since 2016, the even-numbered years, close out the tournament with "[One Shining Moment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Shining_Moment)", performed by [Luther Vandross](249167.md).

### Team awards

Just as the Olympics awards gold, silver, and bronze medals for first, second, and third place, respectively, the NCAA awards the national champions a gold-plated wooden NCAA national championship trophy. The loser of the championship game receives a silver-plated national runner-up trophy for second place. Since 2006, all four Final Four teams receive a bronze plated NCAA regional championship trophy; prior to 2006, only the teams who did not make the title game received bronze plated trophies for being a semifinalist.

The champions also receive a commemorative gold [championship ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Championship_ring), and the other three Final Four teams receive Final Four rings.

The [National Association of Basketball Coaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Basketball_Coaches) also presents a more elaborate marble/crystal trophy to the winning team. Ostensibly, this award is given for taking the top position in the NABC's end-of-season poll, but this is invariably the same as the NCAA championship game winner. In 2005, [Siemens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siemens) acquired [naming rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naming_rights) to the NABC trophy, which is now called the Siemens Trophy. Formerly, the NABC trophy was presented right after the standard NCAA championship trophy, but this caused some confusion.[^cite_note-26] Since 2006, the Siemens/NABC trophy has been presented separately at a press conference the day after the game.[^cite_note-27]

### Most Outstanding Player

After the championship trophy is awarded, one player is selected and then awarded the [Most Outstanding Player](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament_Most_Outstanding_Player) award (which almost always comes from the championship team). It is not intended to be the same as a Most *Valuable* Player award although it is sometimes informally referred to as such.

### Influence on the NBA draft

Because the [NBA draft](835946.md) takes place just three months after the NCAA tournament, [NBA](22093.md) executives have to decide how players' performances in a maximum of seven games, from the First Four to the championship game, should affect their draft decisions. A 2012 study for the [National Bureau of Economic Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Bureau_of_Economic_Research) explores how the March tournament affects the way that professional teams behave in the June draft. The study is based on data from 1997 to 2010 that looks at how college tournament standouts performed at the NBA level.[^cite_note-jr-mm-28][^cite_note-mm-29]

The researchers determined that a player who outperforms his regular season averages or who is on a team that wins more games than its seed would indicate will be drafted higher than he otherwise would have been. At the same time, the study indicated that professional teams do not take college tournament performance into consideration as much as they should, as success in the tournament correlates with elite professional accomplishment, particularly top-level success. "If anything, NBA teams undervalue the signal provided by unexpected performance in the NCAA March Madness tournament as a predictor of future NBA success."[^cite_note-jr-mm-28][^cite_note-mm-29]

## Television coverage and revenues

### Television contracts

Main articles: [NCAA March Madness (TV program)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_March_Madness_\(TV_program\)) and [College Basketball on CBS Sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_on_CBS_Sports)

Since 2011, the NCAA has had a joint contract with [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) and [Warner Bros. Discovery](67824355.md). The coverage of the tournament is split between CBS, [TNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TNT_\(American_TV_network\)), [TBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TBS_\(American_TV_channel\)), and [truTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TruTV) with streaming components [Paramount+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount+) (only CBS games) and [HBO Max](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HBO_Max) (only TBS, TNT, and truTV games).[^cite_note-30]

Broadcasters from CBS, TBS, and TNT's sports coverage are shared across all four networks, with [CBS's college basketball teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_on_CBS_Sports) supplemented with [TNT's NBA teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_on_TNT), while studio segments take place at the [CBS Broadcast Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_Broadcast_Center) in New York City and TNT's studios in [Atlanta](3138.md). In the New York–based studio shows, CBS's [Adam Zucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Zucker) and [Clark Kellogg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clark_Kellogg) are joined by [Ernie Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernie_Johnson_Jr.), [Kenny Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kenny_Smith), and [Charles Barkley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Barkley) of TNT's *[Inside the NBA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inside_the_NBA)* while [Seth Davis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seth_Davis) and [Jay Wright](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Wright_\(basketball\)) of [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) assist with [Adam Lefkoe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Lefkoe) and [Candace Parker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candace_Parker) of TNT's Tuesday night NBA coverage. While three of TNT's NBA voices, [Kevin Harlan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kevin_Harlan), [Ian Eagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ian_Eagle), and [Spero Dedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spero_Dedes) are already employed by CBS in other capacities, TNT also lends analysts [Stan Van Gundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Van_Gundy), [Jim Jackson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Jackson_\(basketball\)), [Grant Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grant_Hill), and [Steve Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Smith_\(basketball\)), secondary play-by-play man [Brian Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Anderson_\(sportscaster\)), and reporters [Allie LaForce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allie_LaForce) and [Lauren Shehadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauren_Shehadi), the latter being from [TBS's MLB coverage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLB_on_TBS), to CBS. In turn, CBS announcers [Brad Nessler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Nessler), [Andrew Catalon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Catalon), and [Tom McCarthy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_McCarthy_\(sportscaster\)) appear on WBD network broadcasts along with analysts [Jim Spanarkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Spanarkel), [Bill Raftery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Raftery), [Dan Bonner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dan_Bonner), [Steve Lappas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Lappas), [Brendan Haywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan_Haywood), and [Avery Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avery_Johnson), as well as reporters [Tracy Wolfson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tracy_Wolfson), [Evan Washburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evan_Washburn), [A. J. Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A._J._Ross?action=edit\&redlink=1), and [Jon Rothstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jon_Rothstein), and rules analyst [Gene Steratore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Steratore). Announcers from other networks like [Lisa Byington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Byington) and [Robbie Hummel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robbie_Hummel) from [Fox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_Sports_\(United_States\)), the latter also working for [Peacock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peacock_\(streaming_service\)) and [Big Ten Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Ten_Network), [Debbie Antonelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debbie_Antonelli) from [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN), [Jamie Erdahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamie_Erdahl) from [NFL Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NFL_Network), and [Andy Katz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Katz) from NCAA.com are also lent to CBS and TNT.

The most recent transaction in 2016 renews the contract through 2032 and provides for the nationwide broadcast each year of all games of the tournament. All [First Four](#First_Four) games air on [truTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TruTV). A featured first- or second-round game in each time "window" is broadcast on CBS, while all other games are shown either on [TBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TBS_\(American_TV_channel\)), [TNT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TNT_\(American_TV_network\)) or truTV. The regional semifinals, better known as the Sweet Sixteen, are split between CBS and TBS. CBS had the exclusive rights to the regional finals, also known as the [Elite Eight](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elite_Eight), through 2013. That exclusivity extended to the entire Final Four as well, but after the 2013 tournament Turner Sports (now TNT Sports) elected to exercise a contractual option for 2014 and 2015 giving TBS broadcast rights to the national semifinal matchups.[^cite_note-DeCourcy_2013-05-07-31] CBS kept its national championship game rights.[^cite_note-DeCourcy_2013-05-07-31]

Since 2014, CBS and TBS split coverage of the Elite Eight. Since 2016 CBS and TBS alternate coverage of the Final Four and national championship game, with TBS getting the final two rounds in even-numbered years, and CBS getting the games in odd-numbered years. *March Madness On Demand* would remain unchanged, although Turner was allowed to develop their own service.[^cite_note-32]

The CBS/Turner broadcasts provide the NCAA with over \$500 million annually, and makes up over 90% of the NCAA's annual revenue.[^cite_note-33] The revenues from the multibillion-dollar television contract are divided among the Division I basketball playing schools and conferences as follows:[^cite_note-revenue_distribution-34]

- One-sixth of the money goes directly to the schools based on how many sports they play (one "share" for each sport starting with 14, which is the minimum needed for Division I membership).

- One-third of the money goes directly to the schools based on how many scholarships they give out (one share for each of the first 50, two for each of the next 50, ten for each of the next 50, and 20 for each scholarship above 150).

- Half of the money goes to the conferences based on how well they did in the six previous men's basketball tournaments (counting each year separately, one share for each team getting in, and one share for each win. Prior to 2026, the Final Four and National Championship games, and prior to 2008, the [play-in game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Play-in_game), did not award units.

  [^cite_note-35]

  &#x20;In 2007, based on the 2001 through 2006 tournaments, the Big East received over \$14.85 million, while the eight conferences that did not win a first-round game in those six years received slightly more than \$1 million each. Most conferences distribute most of the revenue evenly to its member institutions, regardless of performance.

  [^cite_note-basketball_fund-36]

  &#x20;By 2021, the value of the shares or "units" to a conference was worth US\$337,141.

  [^cite_note-37]

  [^cite_note-38]

### History of television coverage

CBS has been the major partner of the NCAA in televising the tournament since 1982, but there have been many changes in coverage since the tournament was first broadcast in 1969.

#### Early broadcast coverage

From 1969 to 1981, the NCAA tournament aired on [NBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_on_NBC), but not all games were televised. The early rounds, in particular, were not always seen on TV.

In 1982, [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_on_CBS) obtained broadcast television rights to the NCAA tournament.

#### ESPN & CBS share coverage

In 1980, [ESPN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_on_ESPN) began showing the opening rounds of the tournament. This was the network's first contract signed with the NCAA for a major sport, and helped to establish ESPN's following among college basketball fans. ESPN showed six first-round games on Thursday and again on Friday, with CBS, from 1982 to 1990, then picking up a seventh game at 11\:30 p.m. ET. Thus, 14 of 32 first-round games were televised. ESPN also re-ran games overnight. At the time, there was only one ESPN network, with no ability to split its signal regionally, so ESPN showed only the most competitive games. During the 1980s, the tournament's popularity on television soared.

#### CBS takes over

However, ESPN became a victim of its own success, as CBS was awarded the rights to cover all games of the NCAA tournament, starting in 1991. Only with the introduction of the so-called "play-in" game (between the 64 seed and the 65 seed) in the 2000s, did ESPN get back in the game starting in 2002 (and actually, the first time this "play-in" game was played in 2001, the game was aired on [The National Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paramount_Network), using CBS graphics and announcers, as both CBS and TNN were both owned by [Viacom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viacom_\(1952%E2%80%932005\)) at the time).[^cite_note-espn-jump-39]

Through 2010, CBS broadcast the remaining 63 games of the NCAA tournament proper. Most areas saw only eight of 32 first-round games, seven of 16 second-round games, and four of eight regional semifinal games (out of the possible 56 games during these rounds; there would be some exceptions to this rule in the 2000s). Coverage preempted regular programming on the network, except during a two-hour window from about 5 p.m. ET until 7 p.m. ET when the local affiliates could show programming. The CBS format resulted in far fewer hours of first-round coverage than under the old ESPN format but allowed the games to reach a much larger audience than ESPN was able to reach.

During this period of near-exclusivity by CBS, the network provided to its local affiliates three types of feeds from each venue: *constant feed*, *swing feed*, and *flex feed*. Constant feeds remained primarily on a given game, and were used primarily by stations with a clear local interest in a particular game. Despite its name, a constant feed occasionally veered away to other games for brief updates (as is typical in most American sports coverage), but coverage generally remained with the initial game. A swing feed tended to stay on games believed to be of natural interest to the locality, such as teams from local conferences, but may leave that game to go to other games that during their progress become close matches. On a flex feed, coverage bounced around from one venue to another, depending on action at the various games in progress. If one game was a blowout, coverage could switch to a more competitive game. A flex feed was provided when there were no games with a significant natural local interest for the stations carrying them, which allowed the flex game to be the best game in progress. Station feeds were planned in advance and stations had the option of requesting either constant or flex feed for various games.

#### Viewing options emerge

In 1999, [DirecTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DirecTV) began broadcasting all games otherwise not shown on local television with its *Mega March Madness* premium package. The DirecTV system used the subscriber's [ZIP code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ZIP_code) to black out games which could be seen on broadcast television. Prior to that, all games were available on [C-Band satellite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-Band_satellite) and were picked up by sports bars.

In 2003, CBS struck a deal with [Yahoo!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahoo!) to offer live streaming of the first three rounds of games under its Yahoo! Platinum service, for \$16.95 a month.[^cite_note-40] In 2004, CBS began selling viewers access to *March Madness On Demand*, which provided games not otherwise shown on broadcast television; the service was free for [AOL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AOL) subscribers. In 2006, *March Madness On Demand* was made free, and continued to be so to online users through the 2011 tournament. For 2012, it once again became a pay service, with a single payment of \$3.99 providing access to all 67 tournament games. In 2013, the service, now renamed *March Madness Live*, was again made free, but uses Turner's rights and infrastructure for [TV Everywhere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Everywhere), which requires sign-in though the password of a customer's cable or satellite provider to watch games, both via PC/Mac and mobile devices. Those that do not have a cable or satellite service or one not participating in Turner's TV Everywhere are restricted to games carried on the CBS national feed and three hours (originally four) of other games without sign-in, or coverage via [Westwood One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westwood_One)'s radio coverage. Effective with the 2018 tournament, the national semifinals and final are under TV Everywhere restrictions if they are aired by Turner networks; before then, those particular games were not subject to said restrictions.

In addition, [CBS Sports Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_Sports_Network) (formerly CBS College Sports Network) had broadcast two "late early" games that would not otherwise be broadcast nationally. These were the second games in the daytime session in the [Pacific Time Zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Time_Zone), to avoid starting games before 10 AM. These games are also available via *March Madness Live* and on CBS affiliates in the market areas of the team playing. In other markets, newscasts, local programming or preempted CBS morning programming are aired. CBSSN is scheduled to continue broadcasting the official pregame and postgame shows and press conferences from the teams involved, along with overnight replays.[^cite_note-41]

#### HDTV coverage

The Final Four has been broadcast in [HDTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HDTV) since 1999. From 2000 to 2004, only one first-/second-round site and one regional site were designated as HDTV sites. In 2005, all regional games were broadcast in HDTV, and four first- and second-round sites were designated for HDTV coverage. Local stations broadcasting in both [digital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_television) and [analog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analog_television) had the option of airing separate games on their HD and [SD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard-definition_television) channels, to take advantage of the available high definition coverage. Beginning in 2007, all games in the tournament (including all first- and second-round games) were available in high definition, and local stations were required to air the same game on both their analog and digital channels. However, due to satellite limitations, first-round "constant" feeds were only available in standard definition.[^cite_note-42] Moreover, some digital television stations, such as [WRAL-TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WRAL-TV) in [Raleigh, North Carolina](57845.md), choose not to participate in HDTV broadcasts of the first and second rounds and the regional semifinals, and used their available bandwidth to split their signal into [digital subchannels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_subchannel) to show *all* games going on simultaneously.[^cite_note-43] By 2008, upgrades at the CBS broadcast center allowed all feeds, flex and constant, to be in HD for the tournament.

### International broadcasts

See also: [List of NCAA Final Four Broadcasters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_NCAA_Final_Four_Broadcasters)

As of 2011, [ESPN International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN_International) holds international broadcast rights to the tournament, distributing coverage to its co-owned networks and other broadcasters. ESPN produces the world feed for broadcasts of the Final Four and championship game, produced using *[ESPN College Basketball](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN_College_Basketball)* staff and commentators.[^cite_note-44][^cite_note-45][^cite_note-usatoday-vitalefinalfour-46][^cite_note-47]

#### All No. 1 seeds in the Final Four



Rank#1 vs. other ranks (prior to 2018)

On two occasions have all four No. 1 seeds make it to the Final Four:

- [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) – [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_Kansas_Jayhawks_men's_basketball_team) (champion), [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_men's_basketball_team), [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team), [Memphis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_Memphis_Tigers_men's_basketball_team)

- [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) – [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024-25_Florida_Gators_men's_basketball_team) (champion), [Auburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024-25_Auburn_Tigers_men's_basketball_team), [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024-25_Duke_Blue_Devils_men's_basketball_team), [Houston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024-25_Houston_Cougars_men's_basketball_team)

#### Final Fours without a No. 1 seed

Four times (including three since the field expanded to 64 teams) the Final Four has been without a No. 1 seed:

- [1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) – No. 2 [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball_team) ([champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_championship_game)), No. 8 [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team) (runner-up), No. 5 [Iowa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_Iowa_Hawkeyes_men's_basketball_team), No. 6 [Purdue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_Purdue_Boilermakers_men's_basketball_team)

- [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) – No. 3 [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005%E2%80%9306_Florida_Gators_men's_basketball_team) ([champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game)), No. 2 [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005%E2%80%9306_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team) (runner-up), No. 4 [LSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005%E2%80%9306_LSU_Tigers_basketball_team), No. 11 [George Mason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005%E2%80%9306_George_Mason_Patriots_men's_basketball_team)

- [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) – No. 3 [Connecticut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9311_Connecticut_Huskies_men's_basketball_team) ([champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game)), No. 8 [Butler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9311_Butler_Bulldogs_men's_basketball_team) (runner-up), No. 4 [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9311_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team), No. 11 [VCU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9311_VCU_Rams_men's_basketball_team)

- [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) – No. 4 [UConn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_UConn_Huskies_men's_basketball_team) ([champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game)), No. 5 [San Diego State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_San_Diego_State_Aztecs_men's_basketball_team) (runner-up), No. 5 [Miami (FL)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Miami_Hurricanes_men's_basketball_team), No. 9 [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Florida_Atlantic_Owls_men's_basketball_team)

Since 1985, there have been 4 instances of three No. 1 seeds reaching the Final Four; 13 instances of two No. 1 seeds making it; and 14 instances of just one No. 1 seed reaching the Final Four. [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) was the first Final Four without a 1, 2, or 3 seed.

#### No. 1 seeds in the championship game

There have been 11 occasions (ten times since the field expanded to 64) that the championship game has been played between two No. 1 seeds:

- [1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – North Carolina beat Georgetown

- [1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1993_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – North Carolina beat Michigan

- [1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1999_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Connecticut beat Duke

- [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – North Carolina beat Illinois

- [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Florida beat Ohio State

- [2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Kansas beat Memphis

- [2015](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Duke beat Wisconsin

- [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – North Carolina beat Gonzaga

- [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Baylor beat Gonzaga

- [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – UConn beat Purdue

- [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) – Florida beat Houston

Since 1985 there have been 18 instances of one No. 1 seed reaching the championship game (No. 1 seeds are 13–5 against other seeds in the title game) and 8 instances where no No. 1 seed made it to the title game.

#### Additional No. 1 seed stats

- In 1997, Arizona became the only team to beat three No. 1 seeds in a single tournament. Arizona (No. 4 seed) beat Kansas in its own southeast region, then beat North Carolina in the Final Four and finally Kentucky in the championship game. The most No. 1 seeds any team can face in the tournament is three (provided that the team itself is not a No. 1 seed, in which case it can only face two No. 1 seeds in the tournament).

- In 2011, the highest seed to advance to the Final Four was No. 3 seed Connecticut, making the 2011 tournament the first time that neither a No. 1 seed nor a No. 2 seed advanced into the final weekend of play. In the same tournament, Butler made history as the first program to make consecutive Final Fours while not being seeded No. 1 or No. 2 in either season.

- There have been 16 teams that have entered the tournament unbeaten. Four of those teams were from UCLA, and all those Bruin teams won each of those tournaments. However, of the other 12 teams entering the tournament unbeaten, just three went on to win the tournament. For details, see [table below](#Undefeated_teams_in_the_tournament).

- In 1980, 1981, and 1982, when the tournament was 48 teams, DePaul was seeded No. 1 but was defeated in the first round.

- Theoretically, a No. 1 seed's most difficult six-game path to win the tournament is to defeat a No. 16, a No. 8, a No. 4, a No. 2, a No. 1, and a No. 1—the highest possible opposing seeds in successive rounds. No No. 1 seed has ever won all six such games, though two teams have won the first five.&#x20;

  - In the 2002 tournament, Maryland reached the final after defeating teams seeded 16/8/4/2/1; they won the tournament after defeating No. 5 Indiana in the final.

  - In the 2015 tournament, Wisconsin reached the final after defeating teams seeded 16/8/4/2/1. In the final, they faced No. 1 Duke with a chance to complete the full six-game path. However, Wisconsin lost the final.

- In 2023, no No. 1 seeds advanced to the Elite Eight for the first time ever. Purdue [lost to Fairleigh Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Fairleigh_Dickinson_vs._Purdue_men's_basketball_game) in the first round; Kansas lost to Arkansas in the second round, and both Alabama and Houston lost in the Sweet Sixteen, respectively, to San Diego State and Miami.
