#### Undefeated teams not in the tournament

The NCAA tournament has dramatically expanded since 1975, and since the expansion to 48 teams in 1980, no unbeaten team has failed to qualify. Since by definition, a team would have to win its conference tournament, and thus secure an automatic bid to the tournament, to be undefeated in a season, the only way a team could finish undefeated and not reach the tournament is if the team is banned from postseason play. As of 2021, no team banned from postseason play has finished undefeated since 1980. Other possibilities for an undefeated team to fail to qualify: the team is independent; the conference does not have an automatic bid; or the team is transitioning from a lower NCAA division or the [NAIA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Intercollegiate_Athletics), during which time it is barred from NCAA-sponsored postseason play in the NCAA tournament or NIT. No men's team from a transitional D-I member has been unbeaten after its conference tournament, but one such women's team has been—California Baptist in 2021 (CBU was able to play in the [women's NIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_National_Invitation_Tournament), which has never been operated by the NCAA).

Before 1980, there were occasions on which a team achieved perfection in the regular season, yet did not appear in the NCAA tournament.

- During 1939, [Long Island University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1938%E2%80%9339_Long_Island_Blackbirds_men's_basketball_team) finished the regular season 20–0 but decided to accept instead an invitation to the second NIT (which they won) instead of the first and only NABC tournament (later called the NCAA tournament), as the NIT was more prestigious at the time. It was not until the mid-1950s that the NCAA required that its tournament would have "first choice" in determining teams for their field. Before then, many of the more successful teams during the regular season chose to play in the NIT instead of the NCAA tournament.

- During 1940, [Seton Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seton_Hall_Pirates_men's_basketball) finished the regular season 19–0, but their record had been built largely against weak teams and thus did not earn them an invitation to the postseason tournament.

- During 1941, [Milwaukee State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Panthers_men's_basketball) finished the regular season 16–0, but their record had been built largely against weak teams and thus did not earn them an invitation to the postseason tournament.

- During 1944, [Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1943%E2%80%9344_Army_Cadets_men's_basketball_team) finished the regular season 15–0 but owing to World War II, the Cadets did not accept an invitation to postseason play.

- During 1954, [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953%E2%80%9354_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team) finished 25–0 and were invited to the tournament, but declined the invitation, due their star players being ineligible due to already graduating.

- During 1973, [NC State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NC_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball) finished the regular season 27–0 and ranked #2 (behind undefeated and eventual tournament champion UCLA) but were barred from participating in the NCAA tournament while on probation for recruiting violations.

- During 1979, [Alcorn State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcorn_State_Braves_basketball) finished the regular season 27–0, but did not receive an invitation to the NCAA tournament. The Braves accepted a bid to the NIT, where they lost in the second round to eventual NIT champion [Indiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978%E2%80%9379_Indiana_Hoosiers_men's_basketball_team).

  [^cite_note-55]

### Repeat champions

Eight programs have repeated as national championships. UCLA is the only program to win more than 2 in a row, winning 7 straight from 1967 to 1973. These programs are:

- [Oklahoma State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_State_Cowboys_basketball): [1945](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1945_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and [1946](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1946_NCAA_basketball_tournament)

- [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball): [1948](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and [1949](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949_NCAA_basketball_tournament)

- [San Francisco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Dons_men's_basketball): [1955](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and [1956](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1956_NCAA_basketball_tournament)

- [Cincinnati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cincinnati_Bearcats_men's_basketball): [1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) and [1962](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament)

- [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball): [1964](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1964_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) and [1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1965_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament); [1967](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1967_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), [1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), [1969](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), [1970](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), and [1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament)

- [Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Blue_Devils_men's_basketball): [1991](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1991_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) and [1992](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_Gators_men's_basketball): [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) and [2007](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- [UConn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UConn_Huskies_men's_basketball): [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) and [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

There have been nine times in which the tournament did not include the reigning champion (the previous year's winner):

- [1978 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978%E2%80%9379_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team) went 19–12 in 1979. They accepted an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_National_Invitation_Tournament), losing to [Clemson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978%E2%80%9379_Clemson_Tigers_men's_basketball_team) in the first round.

- Both [1979 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) [Michigan State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_Michigan_State_Spartans_men's_basketball_team) (12–15) and 1979 runner up [Indiana State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indiana_State_Sycamores_men's_basketball) (16–11) failed to qualify for the 1980 NCAA tournament. Furthermore, neither was invited to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_National_Invitation_Tournament), and Michigan State is the only team to finish the subsequent season with a losing record. Following the 1979 NCAA tournament, Indiana State lost [Larry Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Bird) to graduation, and [Magic Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_Johnson) left Michigan State after his sophomore season to enter the [NBA draft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NBA_draft).

- [1983 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [North Carolina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983%E2%80%9384_NC_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball_team) went 19–14 in 1984. They accepted an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_National_Invitation_Tournament), losing to [Florida State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Florida_State_Seminoles_men's_basketball) in the first round.

- [1986 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986%E2%80%9387_Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball_team) went 18–14 in 1987. They declined an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_National_Invitation_Tournament).

- [1988 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988%E2%80%9389_Kansas_Jayhawks_men's_basketball_team) went 19–12 in 1989. The team was [ineligible for postseason participation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_penalty_\(NCAA\)#Kansas) in 1989 due to NCAA sanctions for recruiting violations.

- Both [2007 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [Florida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_Florida_Gators_men's_basketball_team) (24–12) and 2007 runner-up [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_men's_basketball_team) (24–13) failed to qualify for the 2008 NCAA tournament. Both accepted invitations to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_National_Invitation_Tournament). Florida lost to [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_UMass_Minutemen_basketball_team) in the semifinals, and Ohio State subsequently beat Massachusetts in the championship game to win the tournament.

- [2009 champion](4606216.md) [North Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009%E2%80%9310_North_Carolina_Tar_Heels_men's_basketball_team) went 20–17 in 2010.

  [^cite_note-Goheels.com-56]

  [^cite_note-espn.com-57]

  &#x20;They accepted an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_National_Invitation_Tournament) and reached the finals, losing to [Dayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009%E2%80%9310_Dayton_Flyers_men's_basketball_team).

- [2012 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%9313_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team) went 21–12 in 2013. They accepted an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_National_Invitation_Tournament), losing to [Robert Morris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012%E2%80%9313_Robert_Morris_Colonials_men's_basketball_team) in the first round.

- [2014 champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) [UConn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_UConn_Huskies_men's_basketball_team) went 20–15 in 2015. They accepted an invitation to the [National Invitation Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015_National_Invitation_Tournament), losing to [Arizona State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_Arizona_State_Sun_Devils_men's_basketball_team) in the first round.

### Upsets by low-seeded teams

Main article: [NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament upsets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament_upsets)

#### Best performances by No. 16 seeds

In [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), [UMBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_UMBC_Retrievers_men's_basketball_team) became the first No. 16 seed to defeat a No. 1 seed in the tournament, shocking [Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_Virginia_Cavaliers_men's_basketball_team) [74–54](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_UMBC_vs._Virginia_men's_basketball_game). In [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), [Fairleigh Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Fairleigh_Dickinson_Knights_men's_basketball_team) repeated the feat in a [63–58](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Fairleigh_Dickinson_vs._Purdue_men's_basketball_game) win over [Purdue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Purdue_Boilermakers_men's_basketball_team).

Five other No. 16 seeds have lost to No. 1 seeds by four or fewer points:

- [East Tennessee State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988%E2%80%9389_East_Tennessee_State_Buccaneers_men's_basketball_team) lost 72–71 to [Oklahoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988%E2%80%9389_Oklahoma_Sooners_men's_basketball_team) in [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988%E2%80%9389_Princeton_Tigers_men's_basketball_team) lost [50–49](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Georgetown_vs._Princeton_men's_basketball_game) to [Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1988%E2%80%9389_Georgetown_Hoyas_men's_basketball_team) in [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- [Western Carolina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Carolina_Catamounts_men's_basketball) lost 73–71 to [Purdue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995%E2%80%9396_Purdue_Boilermakers_men's_basketball_team) in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- [Fairleigh Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984%E2%80%9385_Fairleigh_Dickinson_Knights_men's_basketball_team) lost 59–55 to [Michigan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984%E2%80%9385_Michigan_Wolverines_men's_basketball_team) in [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament)

- While [Murray State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989%E2%80%9390_Murray_State_Racers_men's_basketball_team) lost 75–71 to [Michigan State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989%E2%80%9390_Michigan_State_Spartans_men's_basketball_team) in [1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), it is the only No. 16 seed to have taken a game into [overtime](633242.md).

* [Siena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025%E2%80%9326_Siena_Saints_men's_basketball_team) was the first 16 seed to have a double-digit halftime lead on a number 1 seed ([Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025%E2%80%9326_Duke_Blue_Devils_men's_basketball_team) at the [2026 tournament](76465965.md)).

  [^cite_note-58]

### Mid-major teams

Mid-major teams, which are generally defined as Division I teams that do not play in the [American Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Conference_\(NCAA\)), [Atlantic Coast Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Coast_Conference) (ACC), [Big 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_12_Conference), [Big East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_East_Conference), [Big Ten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Ten_Conference) or [Southeastern Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeastern_Conference) (SEC),[^cite_note-Connors-59] are generally underdogs compared to schools in the aforementioned conferences and are often defeated by [power schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_conferences) early in the tournament. However, some mid-major programs experience consistent success despite being at a disadvantage. 1990 champions [UNLV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989-90_UNLV_Rebels_men's_basketball_team) are one such example; then a member of the Big West, they had reached the Sweet Sixteen in four of the last six tournaments prior to their championship. Gonzaga are another example of this, having twice been the tournament runner-up and routinely contending for top-rated prospects.[^cite_note-60]

Every Final Four in tournament history has included at least one power school. The [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) was the most recent edition to have three mid-major programs reach the Final Four: [Indiana State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978%E2%80%9379_Indiana_State_Sycamores_men's_basketball_team) of the [Missouri Valley Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Valley_Conference), [Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penn_Quakers_men's_basketball) of the [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League), and independent [DePaul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DePaul_Blue_Demons_men's_basketball). The [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) had two mid-majors reach the Final Four, those being [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022-23_Florida_Atlantic_Owls_men's_basketball_team) of [Conference USA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conference_USA) and [San Diego State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_San_Diego_State_Aztecs_men's_basketball_team) of the [Mountain West Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_West_Conference). Arguably the edition with the most mid-major success was the [1970 tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1970_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), which had ten mid-majors in the Sweet 16 and six in the Elite Eight. The final four consisted of eventual champions [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969%E2%80%9370_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team) of the Pacific-8 and independents [Jacksonville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969%E2%80%9370_Jacksonville_Dolphins_men's_basketball_team), [New Mexico State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Mexico_State_Aggies_men's_basketball) and [St. Bonaventure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Bonaventure_Bonnies_men's_basketball).

This table shows the performance of mid-major teams from the Sweet Sixteen round to the national championship game from 1939—the tournament's first year—to the most recent tournament.

Notes

- The first column is a list of every mid-major conference. For the conferences that have predecessor names, a footnote (below the table) lists those names and years. Opposite each conference's name are the schools that have appeared in the tournament from the Sweet Sixteen onwards when the school was a member of the conference or a predecessor conference.

- Some of the conferences that are considered mid-majors were regarded as major conferences in the past. For example:&#x20;

  - The Missouri Valley Conference was considered a major basketball conference until many of its most prominent members left in the mid-1970s (before Indiana State's 1979 run to the title game).

  - Conference USA was considered a major conference at its formation in 1995. It arguably became a mid-major in [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_NCAA_conference_realignment), when several of its more prominent teams left for the Big East Conference, and unquestionably became a mid-major during the [early-2010s realignment cycle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9313_Conference_USA_realignment).

  - The WAC was considered a major conference until 1999, when 8 of its 16 members left to form the Mountain West Conference.

  - The MW was considered a major basketball conference until [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010%E2%80%9313_Mountain_West_Conference_realignment), when two of its most prominent basketball programs (BYU and Utah) left for other conferences (West Coast Conference and Pac-12, respectively).

- Certain programs that were members of "mid-major" conferences during deep tournament runs are nonetheless widely viewed as having been major programs at that time. The same applies to many programs that were independent before the 1980s. Examples include (but are not limited to) San Francisco in the 1950s, Marquette in the 1970s, UNLV in the last part of the 20th century, and Gonzaga since no later than the mid-2010s.

#### Defunct conferences and independents

This table shows teams that saw success in the tournament from later defunct conferences, or were independents.

One conference listed, the Southwest Conference, was universally considered a major conference throughout its history. Of its final eight members, seven are now in [power conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Power_conferences) — four in the Big 12, two in the SEC, and one in the ACC. The eighth, Rice, is now in the American, which arguably became a mid-major basketball conference when three of its most prominent athletic brands moved to the Big 12 in 2023, followed by SMU's 2024 departure for the ACC. Another member that left during the SWC's last decade, Arkansas, has been in the SEC since leaving the SWC in 1992. The [Metro Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metro_Conference_\(1975%E2%80%931995\)), which operated from 1975 to 1995, is not listed because it was considered a major basketball conference throughout its history. [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball), which was a member for the league's entire existence, won both of its NCAA-recognized titles (1980, 1986) while in the Metro. It was one of the two leagues that merged to form Conference USA. The other league involved in the merger, the Great Midwest Conference, was arguably a major conference; it was formed in 1990, with play starting in 1991, when several of the Metro's strongest basketball programs left that league.

#### Most teams from different schools taken to the Final Four

[Rick Pitino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rick_Pitino) is the only coach to have officially taken three teams to the Final Four: [Providence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Providence_Friars_men's_basketball) ([1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Providence_Friars_men's_basketball)), [Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball) ([1993](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1992%E2%80%9393_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team), [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1995%E2%80%9396_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team), [1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996%E2%80%9397_Kentucky_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team)) and [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball) ([2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004%E2%80%9305_Louisville_Cardinals_men's_basketball_team)).

There are 15 coaches who have officially coached two schools to the Final Four – [Roy Williams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Williams_\(basketball_coach\)), [Eddie Sutton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eddie_Sutton), [Frank McGuire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frank_McGuire), [Lon Kruger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lon_Kruger), [Hugh Durham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugh_Durham), [Jack Gardner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Gardner_\(basketball\)), [Lute Olson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute_Olson), [Gene Bartow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gene_Bartow), [Forddy Anderson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forddy_Anderson), [Lee Rose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Rose_\(basketball\)), [Bob Huggins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Huggins), [Lou Henson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lou_Henson), [Kelvin Sampson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin_Sampson), [Jim Larrañaga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Larra%C3%B1aga), and [Dusty May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dusty_May).

- [Larry Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Brown_\(basketball\)) took [UCLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979%E2%80%9380_UCLA_Bruins_men's_basketball_team) to the Final Four in 1980, but the appearance was vacated due to NCAA violations. He also took Kansas in 1986 and 1988.

### Point differentials

Point differentials, or margin of victory, can be viewed either by the championship game, or by a team's performance over the whole tournament.

#### Championship victory margins

Widest margin of victory in a championship game

30 points, by UNLV in [1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) ([103–73, over Duke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1990_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game))

Narrowest margin of victory in a championship game

1 point, on six occasions

- [Indiana 69, Kansas 68](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_NCAA_basketball_championship_game) ([1953](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1953_NCAA_basketball_tournament))

- [North Carolina 54, Kansas 53/3OT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game) ([1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament))

- [California 71, West Virginia 70](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game) ([1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament))

- [North Carolina 63, Georgetown 62](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

- [Indiana 74, Syracuse 73](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

- [Michigan 80, Seton Hall 79/OT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

Championship games that went to overtime

Eight times the championship game has been tied at the end of regulation. On one of those occasions ([1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game)) the game went into double and then triple overtime.

- [Utah 42, Dartmouth 40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_NCAA_basketball_championship_game) ([1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944_NCAA_basketball_tournament))

- [North Carolina 54, Kansas 53/3OT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game) ([1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament))

- [Cincinnati 70, Ohio St. 65](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game) ([1961](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1961_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament))

- [Loyola 60, Cincinnati 58](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_championship_game) ([1963](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1963_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament))

- [Michigan 80, Seton Hall 79](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

- [Arizona 84, Kentucky 79](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([1997](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1997_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

- [Kansas 75, Memphis 68](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

- [Virginia 85, Texas Tech 77](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_championship_game) ([2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament))

## Host cities

Main article: [List of tournament venues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament_venues)

### Final Four venues

Main article: [List of Championship games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_champions#Championship_games)

Until 1952, the national championship was played at a separate site from the national semifinal games, which were considered regional finals. Forty-one venues have hosted the final rounds, and several have hosted more than five times:

- [Municipal Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Auditorium_\(Kansas_City\)) in [Kansas City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City,_Missouri) hosted nine times between 1940 and 1960

- [Madison Square Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden_\(1925\)) in [New York](645042.md) hosted seven times between 1943 and 1950

- [Freedom Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Hall) in [Louisville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisville,_Kentucky) hosted six times between 1958 and 1969

- [Caesars Superdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesars_Superdome) in [New Orleans](53842.md) hosted six times between 1982 and 2022

Among cities, Kansas City has hosted the Final Four a total of ten times, with [Kemper Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemper_Arena) hosting in 1988 in addition to Municipal Auditorium. [Indianapolis](57707.md) has hosted a total of eight times between 1980 and 2021 across three venues: [Market Square Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_Square_Arena), the [RCA Dome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RCA_Dome), and [Lucas Oil Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas_Oil_Stadium). The state of Texas has hosted the Final Four eleven times in [Houston](13774.md), [Dallas](53838.md), [San Antonio](53848.md), and [Arlington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlington,_Texas) between 1971 and 2023.

For most of the tournament's history, the national championship game and national semifinal games have been played in basketball arenas. The first instance of a [domed stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome) being used for the Final Four was the [Houston Astrodome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Houston_Astrodome) in [1971](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1971_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament), but the Final Four would not return to a dome until [1982](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) when the [Louisiana Superdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louisiana_Superdome) in New Orleans hosted the event for the first time. The last on-campus venue to host the Final Four was [University Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pit_\(arena\)) in [Albuquerque, New Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albuquerque,_New_Mexico), in [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). The last venue primarily built for a college basketball team to host the Final Four was [Rupp Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupp_Arena) in [Lexington, Kentucky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexington,_Kentucky), in [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). The last NBA arena to host the Final Four was the [Meadowlands Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meadowlands_Arena), then known as Continental Airlines Arena, in [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament). From 1997 to 2013, the NCAA required that the Final Four be played in domed stadiums with a minimum capacity of 40,000. As of 2009, the minimum was increased to 70,000, by adding additional seating on the floor of the dome, and raising the court on a platform three feet above the dome's floor.

In September 2012, the NCAA began preliminary discussions on the possibility of returning occasional Final Fours to basketball-specific arenas in major metropolitan areas. According to *[ESPN.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ESPN.com)* writer [Andy Katz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Katz), when Mark Lewis was hired as NCAA executive vice president for championships during 2012, "he took out a United States map and saw that both coasts are largely left off from hosting the Final Four."[^cite_note-Katz_2012-09-07-88] Lewis added in an interview with Katz,

> I don't know where this will lead, if anywhere, but the right thing is to sit down and have these conversations and see if we want our championship in more than eight cities or do we like playing exclusively in domes. None of the cities where we play our championship is named New York, Boston, Los Angeles, Chicago or Miami. We don't play on a campus. We play in professional football arenas.[^cite_note-Katz_2012-09-07-88]

Under then-current criteria, only eleven stadiums could be considered as Final Four locations.[^cite_note-Katz_2012-09-07-88] On June 12, 2013, Katz reported that the NCAA had changed its policy. In July 2013, the NCAA had a portal available on its website for venues to make Final Four proposals in the 2017–2020 period, and there were no restrictions on proposals based on venue size. Also, the NCAA decided that future regionals will no longer be held in domes. In Katz's report, Lewis indicated that the use of domes for regionals was intended as a dry run for future Final Four venues, but this particular policy was no longer necessary because all of the Final Four sites from 2014 to 2016 had already hosted regionals.[^cite_note-Katz_2013-06-12-89] The policy was changed to only be used if a new venue would be hosting the subsequent tournament's Final Four.[^cite_note-90][^cite_note-91] Under the current policy, the 2030 regionals could be held at [Mercedes-Benz Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercedes-Benz_Stadium) in Atlanta, which opened in 2017, as it will be hosting its first Final Four in 2031 after its planned 2020 hosting was cancelled.

In 2004, the NCAA signed a [memorandum of understanding](1018279.md) with the city of Indianapolis, guaranteeing that the city would host one of five events—men's preliminary games, the men's Final Four, women's preliminary games, the women's Final Four, and the NCAA convention—at least once every five years, in exchange for land to expand the NCAA's Indianapolis headquarters, and for Indianapolis to serve as a designated backup city for the Final Four. This memorandum was stated to be in effect through 2039; in 2014, Visit Indy CEO Leonard Hoops stated that this agreement was only through 2010, and that Indianapolis participates in competitive bids for the events as with other cities.[^cite_note-92][^cite_note-93]

### Home court advantage

On several occasions, NCAA tournament teams have played their games in their home arena. In 1959, Louisville played at its regular home of [Freedom Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Hall); however, the Cardinals lost to [West Virginia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1958%E2%80%9359_West_Virginia_Mountaineers_men's_basketball_team) in the semifinals. In 1984, Kentucky defeated Illinois, 54–51 in the Elite Eight on its home court of Rupp Arena. Also in 1984, #6 seeded Memphis played the first two rounds on its home court, defeating Oral Roberts and Purdue. In 1985, Dayton played its first-round game against Villanova (it lost 51–49) on its home floor. In 1986 (beating Brown before losing to Navy) and '87 (beating Georgia Southern and Western Kentucky), Syracuse played the first 2 rounds of the NCAA tournament in the Carrier Dome. Also in 1986, LSU played in Baton Rouge on its home floor for the first 2 rounds despite being an 11th seed (beating Purdue and Memphis State). In 1987, Arizona lost to UTEP on its home floor in the first round. In 2015, [Dayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_Dayton_Flyers_men's_basketball_team) played at its regular home of [UD Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UD_Arena), and the Flyers beat [Boise State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014%E2%80%9315_Boise_State_Broncos_men's_basketball_team) in the First Four.

Since the inception of the modern Final Four in [1952](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), only once has a team played a Final Four on its actual home court—Louisville in 1959. But through the 2015 tournament, three other teams have played the Final Four in their *home cities*, one other team has played in its *metropolitan area*, and six additional teams have played the Final Four in their *home states* through the 2015 tournament. Kentucky ([1958](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1958_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) in Louisville), UCLA ([1968](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1968_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) and [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) in Los Angeles, [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) in San Diego), and North Carolina State ([1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) in Greensboro) won the national title; Louisville ([1959](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) at its home arena, [Freedom Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_Hall)); Purdue ([1980](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1980_NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament) in Indianapolis) lost in the Final Four; and California ([1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_NCAA_University_Division_basketball_tournament) in the San Francisco Bay Area), Duke ([1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) in Charlotte), Michigan State ([2009](4606216.md) in Detroit), and Butler ([2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) in Indianapolis) lost in the final.

In 1960, Cal had nearly as large an edge as Louisville had the previous year, only having to cross the [San Francisco Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Francisco_Bay) to play in the Final Four at the [Cow Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cow_Palace) in [Daly City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daly_City,_California); the Golden Bears lost in the championship game to [Ohio State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1959%E2%80%9360_Ohio_State_Buckeyes_men's_basketball_team). UCLA had a similar advantage in 1968 and 1972 when it advanced to the Final Four at the [Los Angeles Memorial Sports Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Memorial_Sports_Arena), not many miles from the Bruins' homecourt of [Pauley Pavilion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauley_Pavilion) (also UCLA's home arena before the latter venue opened in 1965, and again during the 2011–12 season while Pauley was closed for renovations); unlike Louisville and Cal, the Bruins won the national title on both occasions. Butler lost the 2010 title six miles (9.7 km) from its Indianapolis campus.

Before the Final Four was established, the east and west regionals were held at separate sites, with the winners advancing to the title game. During that era, three New York City teams, all from [Manhattan](45470.md), played in the east regional at [Madison Square Garden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Square_Garden_\(1925\))—frequently used as a "big-game" venue by each team—and advanced at least to the national semifinals. [NYU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NYU_Violets) won the east regional in [1945](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1945_NCAA_basketball_tournament) but lost in the title game, also held at the Garden, to [Oklahoma A\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1944%E2%80%9345_Oklahoma_A\&M_Aggies_men's_basketball_team). [CCNY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CCNY_Beavers_men's_basketball) played in the east regional in both [1947](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1947_NCAA_basketball_tournament) and [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_NCAA_basketball_tournament); the Beavers lost in the 1947 east final to eventual champion [Holy Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Cross_Crusaders_men's_basketball) but won the 1950 east regional and national titles at the Garden.

In 1974, North Carolina State won the NCAA tournament without leaving its home state of North Carolina. The team was put in the east region, and played its regional games at its home arena [Reynolds Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reynolds_Coliseum). NC State played the Final Four and national championship games at nearby [Greensboro Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greensboro_Coliseum).

While not its home state, Kansas has played in the championship game in [Kansas City, Missouri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_City,_Missouri), only 45 minutes from the campus in [Lawrence, Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lawrence,_Kansas), on four occasions. In 1940, 1953, and 1957 the Jayhawks lost the championship game each time at [Municipal Auditorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_Auditorium_\(Kansas_City\)). In 1988, playing at Kansas City's [Kemper Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kemper_Arena), Kansas won the championship, over Big Eight rival Oklahoma. Similarly, in 2005, [Illinois](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004%E2%80%9305_Illinois_Fighting_Illini_men's_basketball_team) played in [St. Louis](27687.md), Missouri, where it enjoyed a noticeable home court advantage, yet still lost in the championship game to North Carolina.

In [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament), Texas was paired with Mississippi State in Dallas despite being the lower seed. The #6 seeded Longhorns defeated the #3 seeded Bulldogs 68–64 in front of a predominately Texas crowd.

### Previously banned venues

#### South Carolina

The NCAA had banned the [Bon Secours Wellness Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bon_Secours_Wellness_Arena), originally known as Bi-Lo Center, and [Colonial Life Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Life_Arena), originally Colonial Center, in [South Carolina](27956.md) from hosting tournament games, despite their sizes (16,000 and 18,000 seats, respectively) because of an [NAACP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_for_the_Advancement_of_Colored_People) protest at the Bi-Lo Center during the 2002 first- and second-round tournament games over that state's refusal to completely remove the [Confederate Battle Flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederate_Battle_Flag) from the state capitol grounds, although it had already been relocated from atop the capitol dome to a less prominent place in 2000. Following requests by the NAACP and [Black Coaches Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Coaches_&_Administrators), the Bi-Lo Center, and the newly built Colonial Center, which was built for purposes of hosting the tournament, were banned from hosting any future tournament events.[^cite_note-Stubhub.com-94] As a result of the [removal of the battle flag from the South Carolina State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_display_of_the_Confederate_flag#Removal_from_South_Carolina_State_Capitol), the NCAA lifted its ban on South Carolina hosting games in 2015, and it was able to host in 2017 due to North Carolina House Bill 2 (see next section).[^cite_note-95]

#### North Carolina

On September 12, 2016, the NCAA stripped the state of North Carolina of hosting rights for seven upcoming college sports tournaments and championships held by the association, including early-round games of the [2017 NCAA Division I men's basketball tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) scheduled for the Greensboro Coliseum. The NCAA argued that [House Bill 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Facilities_Privacy_&_Security_Act) made it "challenging to guarantee that host communities can help deliver \[an inclusive atmosphere]".[^cite_note-sbnation-ncaaout-96][^cite_note-NYT-NCAA-97] Bon Secours Wellness Arena was able to secure the bid to be the replacement site.[^cite_note-CBSSPORTS-NCAABB-98] In an ironic twist, Durham-based Duke would suffer a defeat to South Carolina in a second-round game at Greenville that year.

## 75th anniversary

Ahead of the 75th anniversary of the tournament, on December 11, 2012, the [NCAA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA) announced the 75 best players, the 25 best teams, and the 35 best moments in tournament history. The NCAA started with a group of more than 100 nominees and then analyzed the tournament statistics for each player to select the 75 finalists from which the public would select the top 15 via an online poll in January 2013.[^cite_note-99]

The results of the public vote were revealed at the [2013 NCAA Final Four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament).[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] Among the 15 players, ten had won a championship, 11 were declared the [Most Outstanding Player](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_I_basketball_tournament_Most_Outstanding_Player) of the tournament at least once, and all made the Final Four at least once. Abdul-Jabbar, Laettner, Lucas, Olajuwon, and Walton all reached the Final Four in every season they played college basketball, and an additional five players went to multiple Final Fours. Hill, Laettner, Russell, and Walton all won two championships, and Abdul-Jabbar won three championships. Lucas and Walton repeated as Most Outstanding Players, and Abdul-Jabbar was declared the MOP all three seasons he played. Bradley, Lucas, Olajuwon, and West were all declared MOP without winning the championship. Twelve players competed in the tournament every year they played college basketball.

UCLA and Duke were the only teams with multiple honorees. Christian Laettner and Grant Hill are the only teammates, they played together for Duke and won two championships in 1991 and 1992. Larry Bird and Magic Johnson competed against each other in the 1979 NCAA championship game, and Patrick Ewing and Michael Jordan competed against each other in the 1982 NCAA championship game as freshmen. Oscar Robertson and Jerry West competed during the same seasons, but never met in the tournament.

Eleven of the players have been enshrined in the [Naismith Memorial Basketball Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naismith_Memorial_Basketball_Hall_of_Fame) and the [College Basketball Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_Basketball_Hall_of_Fame) as players. Michael Jordan and Olajuwon have only been enshrined in the Naismith Memorial as players, and Christian Laettner and Danny Manning have only been inducted into the CBHOF as players. Bill Russell has also been enshrined in the Naismith Memorial as a coach.

Patrick Ewing ([Georgetown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Georgetown_Hoyas_men's_basketball)) and Danny Manning ([Tulsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tulsa_Golden_Hurricane_men's_basketball) and [Wake Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Forest_Demon_Deacons_men's_basketball)) have appeared in the tournament as head coaches. Manning has also recorded six appearances, two Final Fours, one runner-up, and one championship as an assistant for [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kansas_Jayhawks_men's_basketball).

## Popular culture

The NCAA tournament and the [Super Bowl](27718.md) are two notable American sports events that draw both fans and non-fans.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-jensen_08152016-108] Many people are connected to a school in the tournament, having been an alumnus of one of the participants, knowing someone from the college or living close to the school.[^cite_note-jensen_08152016-108]

### Bracketology and pools

Main articles: [Bracketology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracketology) and [March Madness pools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/March_Madness_pools)

There are pools or private gambling-related contests in which participants predict the outcome of each tournament game, filling out a complete tournament bracket in the process. The popularity of this practice grew around [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) when the tournament expanded to 64 games, forming four symmetrical regions with 15 games apiece to decide the Final Four.[^cite_note-109] In 2023, *[Sports Illustrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_Illustrated)* reported that an estimated 60 to 100 million brackets are filled out each year.[^cite_note-PerfectBracket-110] Filling out a tournament bracket with predictions is called the practice of "[bracketology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bracketology)"; sports programming during the tournament often features commentators comparing the accuracy of their predictions. On [The Dan Patrick Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dan_Patrick_Show), a wide variety of celebrities from various fields (such as [Darius Rucker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Darius_Rucker), [Charlie Sheen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlie_Sheen), [Neil Patrick Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Patrick_Harris), [Ellen DeGeneres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellen_DeGeneres), [Dave Grohl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dave_Grohl) and [Brooklyn Decker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brooklyn_Decker)) have posted full brackets with predictions. Former U.S. president [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) began releasing his bracket annually in 2009, his first year in office.[^cite_note-111] While in office, he filled out the men's and women's brackets on ESPN with reporter [Andy Katz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andy_Katz),[^cite_note-112] and they were also posted on the [White House website](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_website).[^cite_note-113] He continued releasing his picks after leaving office.[^cite_note-114]

There are many tournament prediction scoring systems. Most award points for correctly picking the winning team in a particular match-up, with increasingly more points being given for correctly predicting later round winners. Some provide bonus points for correctly predicting upsets, the amount of the bonus varying based on the degree of upset. Some just provide points for wins by correctly picked teams in the brackets.

There are 2^63 or about 9.22 quintillion unique combinations of winners in a 64-team NCAA bracket, meaning that without considering seed number, the odds of picking a perfect bracket are about 9.22 quintillion to 1.[^cite_note-PerfectBracket-110] Including the First Four, the number of unique combinations increases to 2^67 or about 147.57 quintillion.

There are numerous awards and prizes given by companies for anyone who can make the perfect bracket. One of the largest was done by a partnership between [Quicken Loans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quicken_Loans) and [Berkshire Hathaway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berkshire_Hathaway), which was backed by [Warren Buffett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warren_Buffett), with a \$1 billion prize to any person(s) who could correctly predict the outcome of the 2014 tournament. Nobody was able to complete the challenge and win the \$1 billion prize.[^cite_note-115]

### Workplace productivity

During the tourney, American workers take extended lunch breaks at [sports bars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_bar) to follow the game. They also use company computer and internet access to view games, scores and bracket results. Some workplaces block access to sports and entertainment sites but the rise of mobile devices and [live-streamed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Live-streamed) games bypassed those restrictions and even workers not normally in front of computers then had access. Employees spend an estimated average of six hours on the tournament each year.[^cite_note-116] As of 2019, U.S. employers were projected to lose around \$13 billion due to lost productivity during the tournament.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118]

### Tournament-associated terms

As indicated below, none of these phrases are *exclusively* used in regard to the NCAA tournament. Nonetheless, they’re widely associated with the tournament, sometimes for legal reasons and sometimes as part of the American sports vernacular.

#### March Madness

*March Madness* is a registered trademark currently owned exclusively by the NCAA, but it is also a popular term for season-ending basketball tournaments played in March.&#x20;

[H. V. Porter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H._V._Porter), an official with the [Illinois High School Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_High_School_Association) (and later a member of the [Basketball Hall of Fame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basketball_Hall_of_Fame)), was the first person to use *March Madness* to describe a basketball tournament. Porter published an essay named *March Madness* during 1939, and during 1942, he used the phrase in a poem, *Basketball Ides of March*. Through the years the use of *March Madness* increased, especially in [Illinois](14849.md), [Indiana](21883857.md) and other parts of the [Midwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midwestern_United_States). During this period, the term was used almost exclusively in reference to state high school tournaments. During 1977, Jim Enright published a book about the Illinois tournament entitled *March Madness*.[^cite_note-119]

Fans began associating the term with the NCAA tournament during the early 1980s. Evidence suggests that [CBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS) sportscaster [Brent Musburger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brent_Musburger), who had worked for many years in [Chicago](6886.md) before joining CBS, popularized the term during the annual tournament broadcasts. The NCAA has credited Bob Walsh of the Seattle Organizing Committee for starting the March Madness celebration in 1984.[^cite_note-120]

Only during the 1990s did either the IHSA or the NCAA think about [trademarking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trademark) the term and by that time a small television production company named Intersport had already trademarked it. IHSA eventually bought the trademark rights from Intersport, and then went to court to establish its primacy. IHSA sued GTE Vantage, an NCAA licensee that used *March Madness* for a computer game based on the college tournament. During 1996, in a historic ruling, *Illinois High School Association v. GTE Vantage, Inc.*, the [United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Seventh_Circuit) created the concept of a "dual-use trademark", granting both the IHSA and NCAA the right to trademark the term for their own purposes.

After the ruling, the NCAA and IHSA joined forces and created the March Madness Athletic Association to coordinate the licensing of the trademark and investigate possible trademark infringement. One such case involved a company that had obtained the internet [domain name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domain_name) *marchmadness.com* and was using it to post information about the NCAA tournament. During 2003, by *March Madness Athletic Association v. Netfire, Inc.*, the [United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Fifth_Circuit) decided that *March Madness* was not a generic term and ordered Netfire to relinquish the domain name to the NCAA.[^cite_note-121]

Later during the 2000s, the IHSA relinquished its ownership share in the trademark although it retained the right to use the term in association with high school championships. During October 2010, the NCAA reached a settlement with Intersport, paying \$17.2 million for the latter company's license to use the trademark.[^cite_note-122]

Several Division I conferences have used the "Madness" brand, coupled with a non-infringing word, to describe their own tournaments:

- [Big Sky Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Sky_Conference) – Starch Madness. Since 2019, the conference has held its men's and women's tournaments concurrently in [Boise, Idaho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boise,_Idaho), the capital of a state well-known for potato production.

- [Ivy League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivy_League) – Ivy Madness. Like the Big Sky, the Ivies hold their men's and women's tournaments concurrently at a single site.

- [Missouri Valley Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missouri_Valley_Conference) – Arch Madness, used for the men's tournament only. The tournament has been held in [St. Louis](27687.md), whose most iconic landmark is the [Gateway Arch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gateway_Arch), since 1991.

#### Sweet Sixteen

This is a popular term for the regional semifinal round of the tournament, consisting of the final 16 teams. As in the case of *March Madness*, this was first used by a high school federation –in this case, the [Kentucky High School Athletic Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kentucky_High_School_Athletic_Association) (KHSAA), which has used the term for decades to describe [its own season-ending tournaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_Sixteen_\(KHSAA_State_Basketball_Championship\)). It officially registered the trademark in 1988. Unlike the situation with *March Madness*, the KHSAA has retained sole ownership of the "Sweet Sixteen" trademark; it licenses the term to the NCAA for use in collegiate tournaments.[^cite_note-123]

#### Elite Eight

The *Elite Eight* is a popular term to describe the two teams in each of the four regional championship games. The NCAA officially uses the term for the eight-team final phase of the [Division II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_II) [men's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_II_men's_basketball_tournament) and [women's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Division_II_women's_basketball_tournament) basketball tournaments. The winners of these games in the D-I tournament advance to the Final Four (the NCAA does not use the term *Final Four* in D-II). The NCAA [trademarked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trademark) this phrase in 1997. Like *March Madness*, the phrase *Elite Eight* originally referred to the [Illinois High School Boys Basketball Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_High_School_Boys_Basketball_Championship), the single-elimination [high school basketball](3921.md#High_school_basketball) tournament run by the [Illinois High School Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illinois_High_School_Association). In 1956, when the IHSA finals were reduced from sixteen to eight teams, a new nickname for [Sweet Sixteen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NCAA_Men's_Division_I_Basketball_Championship) was needed and *Elite Eight* won the vote. The IHSA trademarked the term in 1995; the trademark rights are now held by the March Madness Athletic Association, a joint venture between the NCAA and IHSA formed after a 1996 court case allowed both organizations to use *March Madness* for their own tournaments.

#### Final Four

The term ***Final Four*** refers to the last four teams remaining in the playoff tournament. These are the champions of the tournament's four regional brackets and remain the only teams on the tournament's final weekend. (While the term *Final Four* was not used during the early decades of the tournament, this term has been applied retroactively to include the last four teams in tournaments from earlier years, even when only two brackets existed.)

Some claim that *Final Four* was first used to describe the final games of [Indiana](21883857.md)'s annual high school basketball tournament. But the NCAA, which has a trademark on the term, says *Final Four* was originated by a *[Plain Dealer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Plain_Dealer)* sportswriter, Ed Chay, in a 1975 article that appeared in the Official Collegiate Basketball Guide.[^cite_note-sportslynx.com-124] The article stated that [Marquette University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquette_Golden_Eagles_men's_basketball) "was one of the final four" of the 1974 tournament. The NCAA started capitalizing the term during 1978 and converting it to a trademark several years later.

During recent years, *Final Four* has been used for other sports besides basketball. Tournaments which use the term include the [EuroLeague](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroLeague) in basketball, national basketball competitions in several European countries, and the now-defunct European Hockey League. Together with the name *Final Four*, these tournaments have adopted an NCAA-style format in which the four surviving teams compete in a single-elimination tournament held in one place, typically, during one weekend. The derivative term *[Frozen Four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frozen_Four_\(disambiguation\))* is used by the NCAA to refer to the final rounds of the [Division I](669931.md) men's and women's [ice hockey](14790.md) tournaments. Until 1999, it was just a popular nickname for the last two rounds of the hockey tournament; officially, it was also known as the *Final Four*.

#### Cinderella teams and Underdog teams

A [Cinderella team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella_\(sports\)), both in NCAA basketball and other sports, is one that achieves far greater success than would reasonably have been best expected.[^cite_note-125][^cite_note-126] In the NCAA tournament, teams may earn the *Cinderella* title after multiple wins in a single tournament against higher seeded teams. The term first came into widespread usage in [1950](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1950_NCAA_basketball_tournament), when the [City College of New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1949%E2%80%9350_CCNY_Beavers_men's_basketball_team) unexpectedly won the tournament in the same month that [a film adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella_\(1950_film\)) of "[Cinderella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinderella)" was released in the United States.

Notable Cinderella teams include [North Carolina State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1982%E2%80%9383_NC_State_Wolfpack_men's_basketball_team) in [1983](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) (the subject of a *[30 for 30](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/30_for_30)* documentary titled *Survive and Advance*), [Villanova](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984%E2%80%9385_Villanova_Wildcats_men's_basketball_team) in [1985](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) (the lowest-seeded team to ever win the tournament), [LSU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985%E2%80%9386_LSU_Tigers_basketball_team) in [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) (the only team to defeat the top three seeds in their region in the same tournament), [UMBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%9318_UMBC_Retrievers_men's_basketball_team) in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) ([the first No. 16 seed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_UMBC_vs._Virginia_men's_basketball_game) to defeat a No. 1 seed), [Saint Peter's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%9322_Saint_Peter's_Peacocks_men's_basketball_team) in [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament) (the first No. 15 seed to advance to the Elite Eight), and [Fairleigh Dickinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Fairleigh_Dickinson_Knights_men's_basketball_team) ([the second 16 seed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Fairleigh_Dickinson_vs._Purdue_men's_basketball_game) to defeat a 1 seed) and [Florida Atlantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022%E2%80%9323_Florida_Atlantic_Owls_men's_basketball_team) (a 9 seed which had never won an NCAA tournament game before its Final Four run) in [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_NCAA_Division_I_men's_basketball_tournament).[^cite_note-127]
